# Windows 11 in Assembly

Aggressive, binary-level superoptimization of a live Windows 11 install — one validated change at a time.

The goal, stated as the "Choice B" target: take the routines this operating system actually runs and
replace them with hand-written assembly that is measurably faster, then prove the win on real hardware
before anything lands here. Over time this accretes into an image whose hot paths are hand-optimized ASM.

This is a research project on **Eylon Krause’s own machine**. It is public as of 2026-09-14, and
it does not redistribute any Microsoft binary — only original assembly, references, tests and
measurements.

---

## What this repository actually is

Each entry under [`changes/`](changes/) is one **optimization unit**: a single Windows-used routine,
reimplemented in assembly, with everything needed to prove it:

```
changes/<NNN>-<name>/
  reference.c      # a plain, obviously-correct scalar reference (the correctness oracle)
  impl.asm         # the hand-written assembly implementation
  correctness.c    # bit-exact / behavior test: impl vs reference vs the SYSTEM function on this PC
  bench.c          # benchmark: impl vs the system function, same inputs, pinned core, warm cache
  RESULTS.md       # the measured numbers on this machine + the disassembly that was compared
  build.bat        # assemble + link + run, reproducible
```

A change is **synced to this repo only after** it passes all three gates on this PC:

1. **Correctness** — bit-exact against the reference across a fuzzed input corpus, and behavior-identical
   to the real Windows function it replaces (same return, same output bytes, same edge cases: empty,
   unaligned, huge, boundary lengths).
2. **Speed** — a real, repeatable win over the shipped system implementation on this hardware. No win,
   or a regression on any measured size class, means it does **not** land. (Same rule the author's NCCL /
   BLIS contributions ran under: never ship a non-win, never regress.)
3. **ABI** — the implementation preserves every Win64 non-volatile register. Run by
   [`tools/abi-check`](tools/abi-check/) (dynamic: sentinels in all of them across a real call) and
   [`tools/abi-audit.py`](tools/abi-audit.py) (static: every `.asm` in the repo).

If a routine can't be beaten, that is recorded honestly in its `RESULTS.md` and the change is not merged.

### Why gate 3 exists

Gates 1 and 2 are structurally blind to one whole class of bug, and it cost this project sixteen
changes. Win64 makes `xmm6`–`xmm15` **callee-saved** — only their low 128 bits; the upper halves of
`ymm6`–`ymm15` are volatile — and sixteen implementations here used them as scratch anyway. Every one
passed both gates: a function that clobbers `xmm6` still returns exactly the right bytes at exactly the
right speed. It corrupts only a *caller* that happened to be holding a `double` there, which no
correctness test comparing integers and strings can observe.

It surfaced by luck. Change 202's benchmark keeps its timing accumulators in `xmm6`/`xmm7`, so a
perfectly correct function reported `0.00 ns`. That was the only symptom, in the only change whose
harness happened to sit on the damage. Gate 3 removes the luck: it fills every non-volatile register
with a sentinel, makes a real call, and reports what did not survive.

All sixteen are fixed, and none of the fixes cost speed — the register pressure was relieved
algebraically rather than by spilling. The recurring trick: a range test written
`(c > LO) AND (HI+1 > c)` needs its second constant as the *first* compare operand, so it has to live
in a register; rewritten `(c > LO) AND NOT (c > HI)` both compares take `c` first, the constants become
memory operands, and the two `AND`s collapse into one `vpandn`. Same instruction count, three fewer
live registers. Several of the rewritten functions came out measurably *faster* than before, because
the constant broadcasts left the prologue (042 `_wcsicmp`: 7.66× → 8.36×).

---

## The validation bench (ground truth for every number here)

See [`docs/PLATFORM.md`](docs/PLATFORM.md) for the full capture. Summary:

| | |
|---|---|
| OS | Windows 11, 25H2, build **26200.8655** (registry `ProductName` still reads "Windows 10" — known stale key) |
| CPU | AMD Ryzen 9 5950X — Zen 3, family 19h, 16C/32T |
| ISA available to emit **and run** | x86-64 + SSE4.2 + AVX + **AVX2** + FMA + BMI1/**BMI2** + POPCNT + LZCNT + ADX + **SHA-NI** + **VAES** + **VPCLMULQDQ** |
| ISA **not** present | **AVX-512** (no), GFNI (no) |
| Secure Boot | ON |
| HVCI / VBS | OFF |
| Toolchain | MASM `ml64` 14.50, MSVC `cl`/`link`, `dumpbin` (VS 2026 Build Tools) |

**Consequence for Choice B:** on this bench the aggressive gains come from AVX2 / BMI2 / VAES / SHA-NI,
not AVX-512. Any implementation intended to be portable off this machine must CPUID-dispatch at runtime
and carry a baseline fallback — an AVX-512 or GFNI path may exist in source but cannot be *validated here*.

### The other benches

A ratio is a statement about hardware, so each machine keeps its own capture and its own results file.
The bench above proved the numbers in `SUMMARY.md`; the others re-prove the same contracts independently.

| bench | machine | what it adds |
|---|---|---|
| #1 | AMD Ryzen 9 5950X (Zen 3) — [`docs/PLATFORM.md`](docs/PLATFORM.md) | the reference bench; every geomean in `SUMMARY.md` |
| #2 | AMD Ryzen 9 8940HX (Zen 4) — [`RESULTS-2ND-PC.md`](RESULTS-2ND-PC.md) | a second AMD generation |
| #3 | Intel Core i9-11900H (Tiger Lake-H) — [`docs/PLATFORM-i9-11900H.md`](docs/PLATFORM-i9-11900H.md) | the first **Intel** bench, and the first with **AVX-512**, **GFNI** and **VBMI2** |

Bench #3 matters for two reasons beyond being one more data point.

**It lifts the AVX-512 restriction stated just above.** That paragraph says a 512-bit path "cannot be
validated here". On bench #3 it can: `AVX512F/BW/DQ/VL/VBMI/VBMI2/VNNI/BITALG/VPOPCNTDQ/IFMA`, `GFNI`,
`VAES` and `VPCLMULQDQ` are all present, along with Intel's `ERMS`/`FSRM` fast `rep movsb` — which is
also a *competitor* at mid sizes that Zen 3 tuning never had to beat.

**It runs a newer Windows.** Bench #1 baselined its disassembly against build 26200.8655; bench #3 runs
26200.9457, with `ntdll`, `ucrtbase` and `kernelbase` all serviced past that point. Because every
`correctness.c` resolves its comparand through `GetProcAddress` against the **live** export, a sweep
there is not a replay of a stored number — it re-proves each contract against newer Windows code than
it was written for.

When two benches disagree, the rule is **fork, do not edit**
([`tools/new-variant.py`](tools/new-variant.py)): the parent's `RESULTS.md` records a measurement taken
on different hardware, and editing the implementation it describes would silently re-attribute that
measurement to a machine that never ran it. Both versions stay in the repository, share the same oracle
and the same gates byte-for-byte, and each says which bench proved it.

To run any sweep on a machine that is not bench #1, start with
[`tools/revalidate-here.ps1`](tools/revalidate-here.ps1): every `build.bat` hardcodes one machine's
Visual Studio path and fails *silently* elsewhere. See [`tools/README.md`](tools/README.md).

---

## Honest constraints (read before assuming "replace the whole OS")

The literal reading — swap every binary in `C:\Windows` for an ASM rewrite and boot it — is **not**
achievable, and pretending otherwise wastes effort. The real, hard blockers:

- **Code signing.** System binaries are catalog-signed. A modified `ntoskrnl.exe` / `win32kbase.sys` /
  driver fails Kernel-Mode Code Signing and will not load while Secure Boot is on; loading a patched one
  means disabling Secure Boot + enabling test-signing, i.e. leaving the supported/secure configuration.
- **No source.** These binaries ship as optimized machine code with no source. "Rewriting in ASM" means
  disassembling, hand-optimizing a *specific routine*, and reassembling with an identical ABI, exports and
  behavior. That is tractable per-routine; it is not tractable for a whole multi-megabyte binary.
- **Servicing overwrites.** Windows Resource Protection + TrustedInstaller ownership + Windows Update will
  revert anything changed in place. Any live patch is temporary by design.
- **This machine has failing RAM** (documented: WHEA machine-checks, BCD-blacklisted pages). It is a poor
  place to run microbenchmarks and a dangerous place to patch boot-path binaries. Benchmarks here are
  treated as directional; publishable numbers want a clean box (the author's Zen4 laptop).
- **Not redistributable.** No Microsoft binary, modified or not, is committed here. This repo holds only
  original assembly, references, tests, and measurements.

### So what "validated here" means in practice

Every change compares our ASM against the **actual function on this PC**, loaded live from its system DLL
(`ucrtbase.dll`, `ntdll.dll`, `kernel32.dll`, …) via `GetProcAddress`, on identical inputs. That is the
strongest honest form of "we replaced what this PC runs and it's faster." **Injecting** the win into the
live system (hot-patch, DLL redirection, or a rebuilt component) is a separate, explicitly-gated step that
requires admin + test-signing and is never done automatically, never on the boot path, never without a
full backup. Correctness-and-speed-proven routines accumulate first; deployment is downstream of proof.

---

## Method

Adapted from the author's upstream-contribution playbook (NCCL, BLIS, nixl, microsoft/STL):

1. Pick a routine Windows actually runs hot. Disassemble the shipped version (`dumpbin /disasm`) and read
   what it does — baseline to beat, and the behavior contract to match exactly.
2. Write the plain scalar reference (`reference.c`) — the oracle.
3. Write `impl.asm` — hand-tuned for the bench ISA, with runtime CPUID dispatch if it's meant to travel.
4. Prove bit-exact vs reference **and** behavior-identical vs the live system function.
5. Benchmark vs the live system function across size classes, pinned core, controlled for the bad RAM.
6. Only a clean win on every size class lands. Record the numbers and the compared disassembly.

Where useful, a second agent (Codex / Gemini) proposes an independent implementation and the two are
judged by the same test+benchmark harness — the tests decide, not opinion.

---

## Live substitution

The landed functions don't just win a benchmark — [`live-substitution/`](live-substitution/) hot-patches
the real `ucrtbase.dll` exports in a running process so calls to them execute our assembly, proves the
results stay identical across a fuzz corpus (with a counter confirming our code ran), then reverts
cleanly. Per-process, runtime, reversible — not a global on-disk DLL swap.

### The 2026-09-20 coverage push — twelve new harnesses, and **seven defects in twenty-two landed changes**

An audit found that only **135 of the 270 LANDED changes** had their export hot-patched anywhere.
Twelve new harnesses took that past **215**, and every one of them compares the **whole destination**
rather than the answer. Seven of them found something:

| what was wrong | changes | why no per-change gate could see it |
|---|---|---|
| `RtlIpv4AddressToString{A,W}` write a **second terminator at index 15** — the end of the 16-character field | 059, 061 | same text, same returned pointer; 17462 of 20000 cases |
| the same, at **index 45** for IPv6's 46-character field | 063, 064 | same text, same returned pointer; all 20000 cases |
| `RtlAppendUnicodeToString` with an **odd `Length`** appends at a *character* boundary, not the byte | 101 | same status, same resulting `Length`; 8255 of 153856 in the follow-up probe |
| `strncat_s`/`wcsncat_s` with a **NULL source and `count == 0`** still validate the destination | 156, 157 | needed an installed invalid-parameter handler to observe at all |
| `CryptBinaryToString{A,W}` set **`ERROR_INVALID_PARAMETER` when `cb == 0`** | 081, 083, 085, 087, 090, 091, 092, 093 | return value, both sizes and every byte matched — only `GetLastError` differed |
| `RtlEthernetStringToAddress{A,W}` write **nothing on a failed parse** — we wrote the groups already read | 119, 120 | status and terminator were right; our partial result reached the caller's buffer |
| the `strtoX` family treat an **invalid base** as a reported error (`EINVAL` + an invalid-parameter report), not a failed parse | 110, 111, 112, 113 | value and endptr agreed; only `errno` and the handler count differed — and without an installed handler an invalid base *terminates the process* |

Every one of those gates was **correct about everything it compared**. That is the whole argument
for this directory: a per-change gate checks the answer, and a live gate checks what the caller's
memory looks like afterwards.

**One finding is deliberately left open**, counted apart from its verdict and printed on every run:
on a *failed* parse the IPv6 entries (121/122) leave the caller's buffer untouched where ntdll
writes partial data — 13822 of 40000 calls, with the status and the terminator agreeing. That is the
*safe* direction, and matching it means reproducing ntdll's abandonment behaviour across a grammar
with compression, embedded IPv4, scope ids and ports. The counter drops to zero when somebody gets
it right.

Two harnesses also **declined to answer questions the implementations decline to answer**: the
crypt32 decoders state that malformed input is out of scope, so their 688-of-1200 damaged-case
divergence is measured and printed rather than failed, and the `CryptBinaryToString` dispatcher's
first draft claimed `CRYPT_STRING_NOCRLF` coverage for two formats that never documented it.


Two of the harnesses also had to be fixed before their results could be trusted, and the same check
caught both — **the post-restore pass differing from the pre-patch pass**, which is impossible if
the harness is measuring the subject rather than itself. A third over-claimed coverage: its
dispatcher routed `CRYPT_STRING_NOCRLF` to formats whose implementations never mention it, reported
2013 differences, and the fix belonged in the harness.

The `CryptBinaryToString` harness is also the first here to **assemble a whole export**: four
changes implement `CryptBinaryToStringA`, one per format, and none of them *is* the export — so a
dispatcher reads `dwFlags` and routes, with a trap stub for the six formats this project does not
implement, asserted never to be entered by the corpus and asserted to decline the two documented
unclaimed flag combinations.

**Swept clean on bench #3 (2026-09-20): 51 of 51 harnesses PASS, 188 individual live-patch proofs,
zero failures.** A "proof" here is one export for which all four of these held in the same run: the
patched prologue began `FF 25` (the jump we wrote); calling the **real** function pointer afterwards
incremented our counter by exactly the number of calls made, so our code executed and not the
shipped code; every one of those results matched the reference and the live export; and after
unpatching the counter froze and the original function worked again. The second of those is the one
that carries the argument — without the counter this would only show that *something* produced the
right answers, which a harness that silently failed to patch would also show.

Reproduce with `.	ools
evalidate-here.ps1 -Only __none__`: `-Only` filters change directories by
substring and `__none__` matches none of them, so the change loop does nothing and the run goes
straight to the ABI audit and the live harnesses.

**Fifty-one harnesses** now cover the landed exports across `ucrtbase`, `ntdll`, `combase`, `rpcrt4`,
`iphlpapi`, `shlwapi` and `kernelbase` — the shlwapi driver proves **33 functions** in a single run and
the kernelbase driver **eighteen**, each one patched, validated against the live export and reverted
byte-for-byte before the next begins. Between them:
the compares, the integer formatters, a transform that writes an upcased output string through the
OS-built case-fold table (`RtlUpcaseUnicodeString`), the Zen-4 variants, the nine `shlwapi`/`kernelbase`
path routines, the bounded `_s` case-fold family (178–181), the bounded `_s` fill family (182–185),
the wide integer parsers (186–191, eight exported names) the two ntdll routines 192–193 and the bounded 64-bit formatter family (194–197).
The `_s` harnesses must install the invalid-parameter handler through **ucrtbase's own** setter and build
`/MD`, or the live export `__fastfail`s the process; the fill harness compares the **whole buffer** on every
case, because that family's error path writes; the wide-parser harness compares **value, `*endptr`
and `errno`** on a corpus deliberately weighted onto the derived character tables, so what it proves
is not only that our assembly is fast but that those tables are the ones ucrtbase actually uses.
The ntdll harness does the same for change 193, whose contract was READ OUT OF THE SHIPPED
DISASSEMBLY rather than derived black-box: it compares the BOOL and the rewritten `*lpi` on every
case, across a corpus weighted onto the two slots that were misread on the first pass.
See its RESULTS.md.
The `lstrcat` pair (228, 230) is proved against **three** guard-page sweeps rather than two, because
`lstrcat` READS the destination before writing it: an unterminated destination is a failure `lstrcpy`
does not have at all, and an implementation that page-clamps only the source and the append passes
every ordinary corpus and still faults there. 230 adds **every destination width in BYTES**, odd and
even, because a clamp that rounds in bytes rather than characters returns the same NULL, faults
nowhere, and leaves exactly one extra byte behind — and it places every one of the 65535 non-NUL code
units in both strings. 142 compares the **returned pointer as an offset** into the buffer when it
points there and as a raw value when it does not, so NULL is never confused with the same answer in a
different buffer.
244 patches **`kernelbase!HashData`**, which is the body BOTH names reach — `shlwapi!HashData` is a
jmp thunk through `api-ms-win-core-url-l1-1-0` into it — and its corpus drives every relative
placement of source against digest inside one buffer, because the fast path holds twelve digest lanes
in registers and that is valid ONLY while the two are disjoint: the shipped inner loop re-reads the
source byte for every lane, so a digest write landing on it changes what the remaining lanes consume.

It turns the parent's mixed-width losses into wins on bench #3 (`2+3` 0.29×→1.47–2.00×, `1234` 0.39×→1.60–1.91×, `rand 32000` 1.01×→13.26×) at **2.57×** geomean on the mixed-width bench and **3.35–3.40×** on the parent's own, LANDS in 5 of 5 runs. **IT ALSO PRODUCED THE SHARPEST CORPUS LESSON IN THE PROJECT**: it passed all 327758 correctness cases while being wrong. `bad32` measured ~9.4 ns at 64, 512, 4000 AND 32000 bytes — flat, i.e. 3379 GB/s, i.e. the input was not read — and probes/classcheck.c proved it returned the correct status and the correct byte count while never writing past wchar 88. The 64-byte "ASCII with rubbish in it" block advanced BOTH cursors by the whole remaining source length instead of by 64, so it wrote 64 correct units and claimed the rest of the buffer as done — and the count looked right *because it is derived from that same cursor*. The old corpus could not fail: every malformed subject was ≤200 bytes with ONE planted byte at src[n/2], so the block meeting it always had <64 bytes left and was always a SHORT block, making a full-block defect structurally unreachable. correctness.c gained a seventh section (one bad byte at EVERY offset of a 300-byte subject × 7 malformed classes, plus recurring bad bytes at 7 periods to 800 bytes) — **331916 cases, and the OLD implementation was rebuilt and run under it to prove the gate FAILS on the defect**, which is change 288's discipline. The parent passes the strengthened corpus unchanged at 3.308×. Every other cursor advance in the file was audited for the same pattern; this was the only instance |

RE-VERDICTED 2026-09-15 by the re-validation sweep, and the reason is not this code: it landed at 8.80x with the 8-byte class an exact 1.00x tie, and on the current Windows build ucrtbase's own _strlwr got 25% FASTER there (6.24 ns -> 4.67) while ours went 6.24 -> 5.75. Every other class improved. Two fixes were measured and rejected and are recorded in impl.asm so they are not retried: overlapping idempotent folds for the tail (correct, but the second fold re-reads what the first just stored — a store-to-load forward on a dependent chain — and measured 8.3 ns), and dropping the prologue broadcasts in favour of memory operands (three memory operands per iteration cost the hot loop 4096 bytes going 63.8 -> 91.6 ns). What WAS kept: the terminator's 32-byte chunk is now folded where it sits and only the bytes before the terminator are stored, instead of falling into a second page check and a second load — geomean 8.74 -> 9.04 |

| [067](changes/067-rtlconvertsidtounicodestring/) | `RtlConvertSidToUnicodeString` (SID→string; length-first decimal conversion, division-free) | `ntdll.dll!RtlConvertSidToUnicodeString` | **LANDED** — **REBUILT FROM THE INSIDE OUT**, 1.42× → geomean **2.86×** over 11 rows (the five-sub-authority account SID that used to BE the benchmark went 1.42× → **3.78×**), worst row 1.10×. It was reopened because change 270 is an envelope over it, and a wrapper can be neither faster nor more correct than what it wraps. **THE FUNCTION IS FIFTEEN NUMBER CONVERSIONS AND A COPY**, and the first version cost A DIVISION PER DIGIT into a scratch buffer that was then read back in reverse — ten dependent 32-bit divisions for a sub-authority like 2596069104 — while declaring the two-digit table changes 054 and 202 use and never referencing it. probes/decimal.c measures 12.77 ns per ten-digit number that way against **3.76 ns** for the form now shipped: divide by 100 with a multiply, know the LENGTH FIRST so the characters go straight into the destination BACKWARDS with no scratch and no reversal, and emit two characters per iteration as one dword store. **NEITHER CONSTANT IS QUOTED**: (v * 51EB851Fh) >> 37 is checked against the compiler's own division and digits10 against its own loop for ALL 4294967296 values of v, zero counterexamples each — the standard change 267 applied to its GF(2) tables, whose first construction WAS wrong. Every table is generated by the assembler from its definition and checked back against it. It is also now a PROC FRAME; the first version was a bare PROC with a 480-byte frame and a `call` in the body, so it had NO UNWIND DATA AT ALL. **THREE THINGS THE OLD GATE COULD NOT EXPRESS, TWO OF THEM LIVE DEFECTS.** (1) Its corpus drew the sub-authority count as `(seed>>8)%%16`, so a count above 15 was NEVER GENERATED ONCE — the live export refuses every one with STATUS_INVALID_SID, the implementation did not, and a SID whose count byte says 200 (which ConvertStringSidToSidW produces in one call, accepting 254) would have been read 1028 bytes out of a 68-byte structure and written ~2500 bytes into a 400-BYTE STACK TEMPORARY. (2) Its overflow sweep STEPPED MaximumLength BY TWO and the other 3000000 cases all used 600, so the boundary was unreachable: probes/oddroom.c shows the room rule is **Length+1, not Length+2**, and that at exactly Length+1 the call SUCCEEDS and writes NO TERMINATOR (the two bytes past the string keep the caller's fill) — both files required Length+2. (3) A SID that is not fully readable is a REFUSAL when its sub-authority array runs off the end and a FAULT when only its identifier authority does: the revision, the count and the array are read under an exception handler and the six authority bytes are not (changes/270-.../probes/truncated.c, swept against a guard page). That handler is the compiler's, in probe.c, for the same reason change 269 CALLS LocalAlloc rather than imitating it. 403339 correctness cases, three-way against the scalar model and live ntdll on the status, Length, MaximumLength AND all 1024 destination bytes including on failing calls, 0 mismatches; 40000 live cases with MaximumLength drawn from AROUND the boundary (5412 of them at exactly Length+1) — and the live harness caught its own corpus first, starting its length at 4 where "S-1-" is EIGHT bytes, reporting zero boundary cases and failing rather than passing; 8 mutants, all caught |

| [097](changes/097-rtlintegertochar/) | `RtlIntegerToChar` (2-digit decimal table; direct MSB-first hex/binary) | `ntdll.dll!RtlIntegerToChar` | **SUPERSEDED by [279](changes/279-rtlintegertochar/)** - it was 1.39x, and it is **WRONG ON EVERY NEGATIVE `length`**. A negative length is not an error and not room: it is a ZERO-PADDED FIELD WIDTH of exactly that many characters. 097's capacity test is `cmp edx, r10d / ja overflow`, an UNSIGNED compare, so a negative length reads as the largest possible room and it writes the digits left-justified with a terminator where the export writes a padded field. Swept against live over every negative length -1..-60 in five bases it differs in **171600 of 171600** cases, including returning SUCCESS and writing to the buffer on calls the export REFUSES with STATUS_BUFFER_OVERFLOW. **Its corpus never asked**: it swept `Length 0..40` and drew its random lengths from `(s>>7)%%40`. 279 is correct on all of them and is 1.54x faster than 097 on positive lengths too, winning EVERY row in a direct A/B (worst 1.01x) |

| [099](changes/099-strncmp/) | `strncmp` byte bounded compare (AVX2 two-regime) | `ucrtbase.dll!strncmp` | **PARKED** — ucrtbase is tuned aligned SWAR; we win ≥128 B (up to 2.06×) but lose the 32 B class |
| [100](changes/100-rtllargeintegertochar/) | `RtlLargeIntegerToChar` (64-bit; 2-digit decimal table + direct hex/binary) | `ntdll.dll!RtlLargeIntegerToChar` | **SUPERSEDED by [280](changes/280-rtllargeintegertochar/)** - it was 1.43x, and it is **WRONG ON EVERY NEGATIVE `length`**, exactly as change 097 was before change 279 replaced it. A negative length is not an error and not room: it is a ZERO-PADDED FIELD WIDTH. 100's capacity test is an UNSIGNED compare, so a negative length reads as the largest possible room. Swept against live over every negative length -1..-70 in five bases across 900 values it differs in **315000 of 315000** cases, including **returning SUCCESS and writing to the caller's buffer on calls the export REFUSES with STATUS_BUFFER_OVERFLOW** - and differing by one byte even at -19, where no padding is needed, because it writes a terminator the export does not. **Its gate never asked**: the corpus swept non-negative lengths only, and all 125050 positive-length cases are correct, so the 1.43x is a positive-length-only number. It is also worst-in-class on a buffer overrun, returning SUCCESS with a wrong answer where both live ntdll and 280 report C0000005. 280 is correct on all of them and is 1.96x faster than 100 on positive lengths too, winning EVERY row (worst 1.13x, binary 6.12x) |

| [123](changes/123-rtlfindlongestrunclear/) | `RtlFindLongestRunClear` | `ntdll.dll!RtlFindLongestRunClear` | **SUPERSEDED by [255](changes/255-rtlfindlongestrunclear/)** - measured **4.33x** of 123 on a shared corpus (448 -> 103 ns). **On that corpus 123 is SLOWER THAN LIVE ntdll** (448 vs 354 ns) despite its 4.79x headline, which is what a size distribution different from its own bench will do to a claim. Settled by measurement in [audits/superseded-duplicates](audits/superseded-duplicates/), not by comparing headline numbers: both changes and the live export were run over one identical corpus, each side in its OWN process (the two implementations export the same symbol name in two of these groups and cannot be linked together), and all three answer hashes were IDENTICAL. **No defect in the older change** - it is correct, and it is retired only so that one export maps to one implementation |

Settled by measurement in [audits/superseded-duplicates](audits/superseded-duplicates/), not by comparing headline numbers: both changes and the live export were run over one identical corpus, each side in its OWN process (the two implementations export the same symbol name in two of these groups and cannot be linked together), and all three answer hashes were IDENTICAL. **No defect in the older change** - it is correct, and it is retired only so that one export maps to one implementation |

| [127](changes/127-rtltimefieldstotime/) | `RtlTimeFieldsToTime` (Y/M/D h:m:s.ms → FILETIME; parse-side of 126) | `ntdll.dll!RtlTimeFieldsToTime` | **LANDED** — **1.54×** (era-based days-from-civil; unsigned-compare validation; year bound 1601–30827 RE'd; 800y sweep + 109k round-trips) |
| [128](changes/128-rtlsecondssince1970totime/) | `RtlSecondsSince1970ToTime` (Unix seconds → FILETIME) | `ntdll.dll!RtlSecondsSince1970ToTime` | **LANDED** — **2.00×** (4 instructions; epoch offset folded through the multiply; full ULONG-domain validation) |
| [129](changes/129-rtlchartointeger/) | `RtlCharToInteger` (ASCII string → ULONG, base 0/2/8/10/16) | `ntdll.dll!RtlCharToInteger` | **LANDED - UNPARKED, AND IT WAS ALSO WRONG** - geomean **1.25x** (median of 15 runs; 1.188-1.263), worst row **1.04x**, ALL 26 ROWS BETTER ON THE MEDIAN. The parse-side complement of landed [279](changes/279-rtlintegertochar/). **THREE THINGS CAME OUT OF UNPARKING IT, AND THE DEFECT IS THE IMPORTANT ONE.** (1) **THE PARK REASON HAD EXPIRED - IT WAS HARDWARE, NOT CODE.** The record said "loses on short inputs", `"42"` at 0.81x, and impl.asm said "Validated on Zen3"; the SAME BINARY on this Zen 4 machine gives 1.00-1.02x on that row. A gate-2 verdict is a measurement on one machine and nothing re-checks it when the machine changes. (2) **THE FOUR-ROW BENCH COULD NOT EXPRESS FOUR REGRESSIONS.** Widened to 26 rows over the input classes this export actually has, four rows came back below parity that the old bench had no way to reach: `0777` base 0 at **0.82x**, `0X10` base 0 at 0.86x, base 8 at 0.96x, `0o7777` at 0.97x. The corpus-cannot-express-the-case lesson applies to gate 2 exactly as hard as to gate 1 - and every row declared `bytes = 8` regardless of its string, so the GB/s column was fiction. ALSO: six consecutive runs of one binary gave THREE LANDS AND THREE PARKED, because at ~3 ns/call the difference under test is ~0.2 ns; the verdict is now the MEDIAN OF 15 RUNS PER ROW with the spread reported, since reading it off the last run is picking the answer. (3) **THE DEFECT: IT DID NOT STEP OVER A LEADING NUL, AND THE EXPORT DOES.** Gate 4 refused to pass - 291 differences of 40000, every one a string starting with a terminator, live returning 6, 7, 3, 0x08225F8F, 0xB26A432B where ours returned 0. probes/pastnul.c + pastnul2.c mapped 23 planted-byte cases and ONE rule explains all of them: **if byte 0 is NUL, step over it and parse from byte 1** - one NUL, index 0 only. `00 36`->6, `00 2D 36`->-6, `00 30 78 31 66`->0x1F, `00 30 37 37 37`->777 (still DECIMAL); but `00 00 36`->0 and `20 00 38`->0, so a second NUL stops it and a leading SPACE is not double-skipped. Almost certainly an off-by-one in the shipped end test, since the skip is a SIGNED compare against `' '` and 00 satisfies it - and a drop-in has to reproduce it, overread and all. **WHY THREE GATES MISSED IT FOR THE WHOLE LIFE OF THE CHANGE**: the no-digit cases were string LITERALS, so what follows the terminator is whatever the linker put there and it happened to yield 0 - exactly what we returned - AND reference.c inherited the rule FROM impl.asm rather than from the export, so the three-way comparison was structurally blind (change 288 hit the identical shape one commit earlier: an assumption held by both sides of a comparison is invisible to comparing them). Gate 4 caught it only because ITS CASE BUFFER IS A REUSED STATIC ARRAY with real bytes after the terminator. correctness.c gained a PLANTED-BYTES section: all 23 patterns, a planted digit at every position 1..23 behind a NUL, and a leading NUL in front of all 256 byte values. **THE FIRST TWO PERFORMANCE ATTEMPTS WERE NET LOSSES AND THE REASON IS WORTH KNOWING**: geomean 1.172->1.044 and ->1.100, with the DECIMAL rows going 6.88->8.99 ns - rows that execute NONE of the edited code. Two independent edits to a COLD path each regressed a HOT path they cannot logically touch: that is code LAYOUT, not instruction count. (My first note blamed the comment block, which is wrong - comments emit no bytes.) The fix that unlocked everything was `ALIGN 16` before the digit loop, which changes no instruction path, stabilised `dec 10 digits` at 6.71 ns, and makes the loop alignment INDEPENDENT OF THE CODE SIZE BEFORE IT - after which the prefix window (three compares -> one biased unsigned window test), dropping a reload of a byte `al` already held, letting auto-detected bases skip the validity ladder (a base we chose is valid by construction; only a CALLER-supplied one can be wrong - this is what brought the last two rows over parity), and validating that base by range+bitmask instead of a four-compare ladder all paid. 39 mutants: 34 caught, 5 PROVED EQUIVALENT, and **1 WAS A REAL GATE HOLE** - #22 made the base range test SIGNED and survived, because `bt` TAKES ITS BIT INDEX MODULO 32, so base 0x80000002 indexes bit 2 (a SET bit) and only the unsigned test keeps it out; the sweep's largest base was 0xFFFFFFFF, low five bits 31 and clear, so the hole was invisible. Five aliasing bases added to both gates and #22 is now caught by both - which exposed a second instance of the same defect: the fuzz drew `BASES[(seed>>5)%%16]` with a HARD-CODED count, so appended bases would have sat in the array and never been drawn. Gate 3 and gate 4 did not exist when this change was written and are both new here (T_129 with CALL4 and a real writable ULONG so "did not write" differs from "wrote and faulted"; 40000 live cases, 0 differ, with twelve coverage assertions and *Value pre-poisoned because zeroing the output before discovering an invalid base is the natural way to write it and would be wrong). The genuine win is the base-0/base-16 prefix path at **1.86-1.95x**, where the export costs ~13 ns against our ~7 - nearly double its own decimal path; the rest is 1.04-1.43x, because this incumbent was already efficient |

Size-dispatched fill (SSE2 / AVX2 / `rep stosd`) with each boundary measured; no bounds check in the contract |

The fuzz alphabet contained no space, so the oracle, the implementation and the test were all wrong together. Caught while probing the narrow sibling (change 217); the amendment was verified over 2015539 strings on each of two alphabets (0 mismatches for BOTH exports). correctness.c now ENUMERATES all 335923 strings over {a,'.',backslash,'/',':',space} of length 0..7 rather than sampling them, and the change is now driven live for the first time, together with 217 |

| [140](changes/140-pathremoveextensionw/) | `PathRemoveExtensionW` (truncate at the extension) | `shlwapi.dll!PathRemoveExtensionW` | **LANDED** — **2.98×** (up to **8.27×**; reuses 132's scan, **plus a MAX_PATH guard 132 does not have** — ≥260 chars is a no-op, found by fuzzing). **CORRECTED 2026-09-15 — HAD SHIPPED WRONG:** it reused change 132's extension rule, which was missing the SPACE stopper, and was wrong on **57746 of 349525** strings over {a,'.',backslash,space} (0 after the fix). Found by discovery/extension_space_audit.c after the same bug surfaced in 132 while probing change 217. correctness.c now ENUMERATES that alphabet rather than sampling it, and is verified protective — reverting the implementation makes it fail immediately on ". ". |
| [141](changes/141-pathremoveblanksw/) | `PathRemoveBlanksW` (strip leading/trailing spaces) | `shlwapi.dll!PathRemoveBlanksW` | **LANDED** — **4.23×** (up to **10.84×**; space-only, **no** MAX_PATH guard unlike 140, and move-then-terminate — the reverse of 139) |
| [142](changes/142-pathaddbackslashw/) | `PathAddBackslashW` (append a trailing backslash) | `shlwapi.dll!PathAddBackslashW` | **LANDED** — bit-exact (returned pointer *and* whole buffer); **4.51–4.63×** geomean over ten consecutive runs, up to **8.39×**, worst class **2.09×**. RE'd the result-fits-in-260 rule *and its ordering* (long already-terminated paths return NULL). UNPARKED WITHOUT TOUCHING THE IMPLEMENTATION: the benchmark was wrong twice over. It undid the in-place edit with a `memcpy` OF THE WHOLE PATH, which at 16 characters costs as much as the function — the restore was REPLACING the measurement — and the single store that actually suffices then landed on the buffer the NEXT call was about to load 32 bytes from, where a wide load cannot forward from a just-retired narrow store and waits for it to drain while the shipped scalar loop forwards cheaply. One store on a ROTATED buffer: 0.85× → **2.13×** at 16 chars, 3.13× → 4.63× geomean, same asm. The row's own evidence had said so all along — our time was FLAT in the input (13.06 ns at 16 chars, 13.26 at 64), and *a row whose time does not vary with the input is not measuring the input*. The bench now asserts per row whether the call writes at all, so a restore can never again be timed where nothing needs restoring. Adding its ABI case also exposed a hole in gate 3: a crashing driver exits with an NTSTATUS, which `cmd` reads as NEGATIVE, so `if errorlevel 1` was false and the gate printed PASS with this change silently absent — now compared against zero |

| [163](changes/163-pathremovefilespecw/) | `PathRemoveFileSpecW` | `shlwapi.dll!PathRemoveFileSpecW` | **PARKED — and now for a different reason.** RE-EXAMINED with the disassembly after that technique unparked [167]: the verdict is unchanged but the reason is now ECONOMIC rather than EPISTEMIC. **The contract is no longer a mystery** — `PathRemoveFileSpecW` is an envelope over FIVE named exports (`PathCchSkipRoot`, `wcschr` in a last-backslash loop, `PathCchIsRoot`, `PathIsUNCW`, and `PathCchRemoveFileSpec`, which is **[240], landed**). It is blocked on `PathCchSkipRoot`, a full root parser — **and that parser is worth NOTHING to optimise on its own: measured at 5.16\u20137.62 ns and FLAT in the input** (5.16 ns on a 250-char path against 5.21 on a short one), because it only ever looks at the root; its ceiling is call overhead. Meanwhile **`PathRemoveFileSpecW` is already 0.670 ns/char** (167.47 ns net of the restore on 250 chars), the same band as `PathCanonicalizeW` (0.65) near the BOTTOM of the survey. So deriving a root parser that yields no speed to reach a function that is already fast per character is not the best available work. The old note’s "leading backslash runs are non-monotonic, so no single rule fits" is now explained: that is `PathCchSkipRoot`’s UNC walk (`\\` + server + `\` + share + `\`), where the separator after the SERVER is consumed even when the server is empty and the one after the SHARE only when it is not — `\`→1, `\\`→2, `\\\`→3, `\\\\`→3 and 3 for every longer run. **THE PRIZE BEHIND THE SAME BLOCKER IS `PathIsSameRootW`** — 5.73 ns/char, the third-highest per-byte cost in the whole survey — which the disassembly shows is `PathSkipRootW(a) != NULL && (PathSkipRootW(a) - a) <= PathCommonPrefixW(a,b,NULL) + 1`, and `PathCommonPrefixW` is **[167], landed**; the root skip itself is `PathCchSkipRoot` plus one rule measured over 1365 strings with 0 disagreements (`a root of exactly 2 ending in ":" → NULL`). Probes committed: `skiproot.c` (the keystone dumped exhaustively) and `cost.c` (the measurement that re-verdicted this) |

Safe to vectorise because `RtlUpperChar` was verified over all 256 values to be the plain ASCII rule — no locale table exists to reproduce) |
| [166](changes/166-rtlipv6stringtoaddressw/) | `RtlIpv6StringToAddressW` (wide IPv6 parse) | `ntdll.dll!RtlIpv6StringToAddressW` | **LANDED** — **3.84–3.89×** (was 3.62×; **revised while landing [250]**, which found a **0.79× regression on `::`** — a byte-loop zero-fill and a dead shift, both fixed — and an **overstated correctness claim**: the harness compares the address only on SUCCESS, and the shipped export leaves partial bytes on **17268** failing calls where this one leaves none. The obvious fix measured WORSE (17268→18240, inverted) and is recorded, not applied.) Originally **3.62×** (up to **6.84×**; the form [121] had scoped out as needing "the CRT/OS Unicode digit table". Swept over all 65536 units the digit set is exactly **17 contiguous blocks of ten** — the frozen Unicode 3.0 `Nd` list. The scan is ASCII-only and fixes `seen`/`*Terminator`; the *value* is a separate re-parse of the token that honours `0x` and those blocks, and runs past where the scan stopped. Found and fixed **four defects in the landed 121**) |
| [167](changes/167-pathcommonprefixw/) | `PathCommonPrefixW` (longest common path prefix) | `shlwapi.dll!PathCommonPrefixW` | **LANDED** — **15.0–16.0×** geomean (up to **26.3×**), worst class **8.50×**. **UNPARKED.** It sat at **99.3 %** — 784 of 116281 exhaustive pairs resisted every rule fitted from the outside — and the old conclusion was that the residuals were mutually contradictory, that reproducing it "requires reproducing that root parser first", and that the next step was to derive `PathSkipRootW`. **All three were wrong, and the disassembly says so in about twenty instructions.** THERE IS NO ROOT PARSER: `PathSkipRootW` is never called, and the entire root handling is two inline tests for a DOUBLED leading backslash plus a four-instruction `is_unc(p) = p[0]=='\\' && p[1]=='\\'`. AND THE NOTORIOUS "+1" IS THREE INSTRUCTIONS — `sub rsi,r14 / mov ebp,3 / sar rsi,1 / cmp esi,2 / cmovne ebp,esi` — so **ANY computed length of exactly 2 is reported as 3**, nothing to do with drive letters, colons or identical strings. Those two facts settle all four recorded residuals (`\` vs `\\`→0 because f2 is UNC and f1 is not; `\\` vs `\\\`→3 because both skip 2 and the boundary lands at f1+2; `\\` vs `\\a`→0 because the component lengths differ; `\a` vs `\a\`→3 because a NUL against a `\` ends both components at the same length). The same 116281-pair corpus now passes with **ZERO** mismatches. THE IMPLEMENTATION IS A DIFFERENT FORMULATION FROM ITS ORACLE ON PURPOSE: the shipped loop walks component by component and `reference.c` transcribes that literally, while `impl.asm` does a single LOCKSTEP walk because that is what vectorises — agreement between them is evidence the equivalence argument holds rather than evidence one model was compiled twice. THE FOLD IS NOT ASCII (947 units differ), so the vector loop compares RAW units and consults change 210's OS-built `RtlUpcaseUnicodeChar` table only where they differ; a path differing from its partner ONLY IN CASE then advanced one character per block and measured **903.55 ns (2.44×)**, this implementation's own worst row, fixed by applying an ASCII fold to the block ONLY once the raw compare has already failed — free on the identical rows, can never produce a false MATCH, and a false mismatch is settled exactly by the table. That row became **86.00 ns (26.29×)**, the best in the table, and the geomean went 11.14× → 15.0–16.0×. The `254, 1 component` row is `254 identical` with the separators removed and isolates what the shipped function pays PER COMPONENT: its time nearly halves (1487→680 ns) while ours does not move (76.4→76.8). Gate 1: **407443** cases three-way bit-exact, both the int and the whole buffer. Gate 4: **116907** cases live, driven through the shlwapi name while only the kernelbase body is patched, so the counter proves both that our code ran and that shlwapi reaches that body |

Contract: `strlcpy`-shaped not `strncpy`-shaped — no NUL fill — and `cchMax` is signed, so 0 or negative writes *nothing at all*. Confirmed first try, 2M fuzz, 0 mismatches) |

The NUL test must come FIRST — so `wMatch==0` is *always* NULL; the reverse order was refuted on 210697/3M cases) |

| [171](changes/171-pathremovebackslashw/) | `PathRemoveBackslashW` (strip one trailing backslash) | `shlwapi.dll!PathRemoveBackslashW` | **LANDED** — **2.10x** (up to 5.81x; returns a pointer to the LAST CHARACTER, not the terminator — that off-by-one was refuted on 1451150/2M cases with otherwise-identical buffers. | [172](changes/172-pathquotespacesw/) | `PathQuoteSpacesW` (wrap a path containing a space in quotes) | `shlwapi.dll!PathQuoteSpacesW` | **LANDED** — **2.06x** (up to 5.97x; ONE pass finds the length and the space together, with `tzcnt` of an empty mask (=32) deciding "space after terminator" for free. "Space" is EXACTLY U+0020 — 1 of 65535 code units, tab does not count — and the MAX_PATH rule is n<=257, both pinned by sweep. Landing it needed a size-laddered overlapping move AND narrow 16-byte probes, because a 32-byte aligned load cannot store-forward from the caller's recent narrow writes) |
| [173](changes/173-pathfindnextcomponentw/) | `PathFindNextComponentW` (next path component) | `shlwapi.dll!PathFindNextComponentW` | **LANDED** — **3.98x** (up to 8.05x, ~70 GB/s; ONE pass locates the separator and the terminator together. Separator swept to exactly U+005C — the sixth distinct separator convention found in this DLL) |
| [174](changes/174-pathundecoratew/) | `PathUndecorateW` (remove a `[n]` decoration) | `shlwapi.dll!PathUndecorateW` | **LANDED** — **3.90x** (up to 8.02x; ONE forward pass yields the length, the last backslash AND the last dot from three vpcmpeqw per block, using bsr for last-match and taking two broadcast constants as VEX MEMORY operands because only ymm0-ymm5 are volatile. The contract took three rounds: the group must hug the LAST dot, not merely be followed by one — "a[1].b[2].c" undecorates to "a[1].b.c", refuting the obvious rule on 171989 then 29053 of 2M cases. Empty brackets DO count; the '[' may not start the component) **CORRECTED 2026-09-15 — HAD SHIPPED WRONG:** conjunct (b) — the `]` must hug the component's LAST dot — is an extension position by another name and carried change 132's missing SPACE stopper. This change never cited 132; it derived its own rule and fuzzed it over 2M cases over an alphabet with NO SPACE IN IT, which is exactly why neither its own corpus nor the first audit could see it. The structural sweep found it: wrong on **1634 of 335923** strings, 0 after the fix; smallest case ". []" → ". ", left alone. THE TWO USES OF THE BACKSLASH HAD TO BE SEPARATED — it delimited the COMPONENT for conjunct (d) *and* bounded the extension search for (b), and only the second takes the space, so the scan now tracks `comp` and a second position `stop` in a pushed rbx. AND THE CORRECTION MADE IT FASTER: the fourth compare per block cost 81.0 → 98.9 ns at 1024 chars until the loop was restructured around it (the common block holds none of backslash/dot/space/NUL, so one vpor + one vpmovmskb answers for all three at once — fewer uops than before the stopper existed), landing at **65.1 ns**, better than it originally shipped. Its live-substitution corpus, which had passed every session while the change was wrong, is now EXHAUSTIVE over an alphabet containing a space. |

The contract needed a cell-level probe: it writes MORE THAN ONE cell ("ab   c" writes cells 2 AND 4, the LAST space of the run), and with no unquoted space it falls back to a TRAILING-BLANK TRIM — which is why `"`+space is cut but `"`+space+`a` is not. The obvious rule was refuted on 265039/2M) |

Its drive-letter set is IDENTICAL to 171's across all 65535 code units, so two DLLs share one table. Unlike change 143's [1,32768], this one has NO upper bound on cchPath. Landed only after a narrow SWAR probe fixed 0.94x/0.83x on the short classes) |
| [177](changes/177-pathisprefixw/) | `PathIsPrefixW` (is one path a prefix of another) | `shlwapi.dll!PathIsPrefixW` | **LANDED** — **28.5–29.2×** geomean (up to **81.4×**), worst class **1.72×**. **UNPARKED.** It was never parked as "we could not derive it" but as **"we derived it, and it is `PathCommonPrefixW`, which is parked"** — the identity `PathIsPrefixW(pre,path) == (PathCommonPrefixW(path,pre,NULL) == wcslen(pre))`, fuzz-verified against BOTH live exports. [167] has now landed, so this function's ENTIRE BODY is two other landed changes: **167 for the walk, [001] for the length**. The identity is re-established from scratch rather than inherited (**516441** cases, 0 mismatches) and it explains every odd probe result, above all the trailing backslash: `C:\a\` is NOT a prefix of `C:\a\b`, because the common prefix is 4 against a `wcslen` of 5 — and `C:` is not a prefix of `C:\a` while `C:\` IS, which is change 167's "a computed length of exactly 2 reports 3" rule showing through from underneath. TWO THINGS THE IDENTITY CANNOT SETTLE, both measured: **NULL** (wcslen(NULL) is not evaluable, and every NULL combination is FALSE — including `L""` against a NULL path, which would have been the plausible TRUE); and the ARGUMENT ORDER, which turns out to be a **red herring** — with achPath NULL, PathCommonPrefixW is SYMMETRIC in its first two arguments, so swapping them changes nothing on all 116281 pairs. **THE BENCHMARK CAUGHT THE ONE REAL MISTAKE**: the first version computed `wcslen(pszPrefix)` with a scalar loop on the reasoning that the prefix is "the short one" — false exactly when the two paths diverge early, where the walk stops at once and the length scan becomes the WHOLE function. The `254, differ at 8` row measured **57.51 ns (1.67×)** in a table where everything else was above 11×, every nanosecond of it a scalar loop over a prefix already abandoned; composing change 001 took that row to **8.25 ns (11.68×)**, `254 true` to **19.14 ns (74.85×)**, and the geomean from **11.00× to 28.5–29.2×**. Nothing NEW is vectorised here — the walk is 167's and the length is 001's — but BOTH had to be. The observable is a single BOOL, so the corpus is weighted towards TRUE answers on purpose: a corpus of random path pairs is almost entirely FALSE and a constant-FALSE implementation would pass it. Gate 1: **431907** cases three-way, **44878** of them TRUE. Gate 4: **117196** cases live, 948 TRUE and 301 with a trailing separator — the shape that does all the work and still answers FALSE |

Both outcomes fill, so one count feeds one vpbroadcastb loop) |
| [183](changes/183-wcsset-s/) | `_wcsset_s` (bounded wide fill) | `ucrtbase.dll!_wcsset_s` | **LANDED** — **1.99x** (up to 3.54x, ~33 GB/s; wide mirror of 182, fuzzed in its own right in the same probe run — 1M cases, 0 mismatches — because byte/wide pairs in this CRT are not automatically identical. Lower ratio than 182 for a measured reason: ucrtbase's wide fill already covers twice the memory per store, so the scalar baseline is ~2x faster per byte; our two implementations are within 4 % of each other in GB/s) |
| [184](changes/184-strnset-s/) | `_strnset_s` (bounded byte fill with count) | `ucrtbase.dll!_strnset_s` | **LANDED** — **2.99x** (up to 9.06x; bounded form of change 078, with TWO independent limits. Rule confirmed on the FIRST candidate, 1M cases: fill min(count, numberOfElements-1) then empty on the error path, min(count, length) on success, and `_TRUNCATE` is NOT special-cased — it just saturates. THE SCAN CANNOT BE CLIPPED BY count, because even at count 0 the return value still depends on whether a terminator exists inside the bound; only the fill is clipped, by one cmova. Correctness sweeps length x EVERY bound x EVERY count, ~1.8M triples) |

| [186](changes/186-wtoi/) | `_wtoi` (= `_wtol`; wide decimal parse) | `ucrtbase.dll!_wtoi` | **LANDED** — **2.78x** (up to 5.95x). **This function was explicitly SCOPED OUT of this repo and the decision is now overturned.** changes/109-atoi64/RESULTS.md said a bit-exact reimpl "would need the CRT's full Unicode digit table, not an ASCII loop". Two sweeps replaced that assumption with a measurement: over all 65536 code units the digit set is EXACTLY 18 CONTIGUOUS BLOCKS OF TEN (180 members, 18 ascending runs, no exceptions) — the Unicode 3.0 Nd list, the SAME set change 166 found frozen in ntdll, re-measured here in a different DLL — and it is byte-identical under LC_ALL = C/en-US/ar-SA/ja-JP/th-TH/hi-IN/de-DE.UTF-8/.65001, so a fixed table is honest rather than C-locale-only. Whitespace is 26 units (the byte form skips six) and U+200B is NOT one. `_wtoi` and `_wtol` share one code address. Classifier split by frequency: ASCII in one lea/cmp, `c < 0x660` rejects everything else including NUL, fullwidth in three instructions, the other 16 blocks in ONE AVX2 pass (vpminuw+vpcmpeqw, xmm-only so no vzeroupper anywhere) |
| [187](changes/187-wtoi64/) | `_wtoi64` (wide 64-bit parse) | `ucrtbase.dll!_wtoi64` | **LANDED** — **2.23x** (up to 3.05x; the 64-bit result behaviour was RE-MEASURED rather than inherited from change 109 — byte and wide forms in this CRT have diverged before. Saturates at _I64_MAX/_I64_MIN, and a magnitude of exactly 2^63 IS accepted on the negative side, so -9223372036854775808 is exact. The 2^63 cap needs no register at all: "acc >= 2^63" is exactly "the top bit is set", which the sign flag answers for free) |
| [188](changes/188-wcstol/) | `wcstol` (wide base-0/2..36 parse) | `ucrtbase.dll!wcstol` | **LANDED** — **1.89x** (change 110's structure crossed with change 186's sets, and the CROSSING is where the traps are: non-ASCII digits work in EVERY base (U+0667=7 parses in base 8, U+0668=8 does not); the base>10 letters are ASCII-ONLY even though the digits are not (fullwidth 'f' is not a hex digit but fullwidth '9' is a decimal one); **the "0x" prefix zero may be ANY block's zero** — `<U+0660>x1f` really is 31 — which refuted the natural L'0' port on 1579/1.5M; but the 'x' itself stays ASCII-only. An invalid base raises the handler once, sets EINVAL and writes *endptr=nptr. SHAPE WAS MEASURED: a classifier-per-character CALL was bit-exact but regressed two classes (0.95x/0.85x); inlining ASCII digit, then letter, then fullwidth, then the blocks took it 1.16x -> 1.89x, and the Arabic-Indic class was ADDED to the bench first so the trade-off would show) |
| [189](changes/189-wcstoul/) | `wcstoul` (wide unsigned parse) | `ucrtbase.dll!wcstoul` | **LANDED** — **1.90x** (up to 2.39x; 188's body with change 111's unsigned tail: '-' negates modulo 2^32 with no error, and the limit is 2^32-1 REGARDLESS of sign where 188's moves with it. Both were fuzzed in the SAME run, 1.5M each, and the same run refuted the ASCII-only-prefix-zero variant for BOTH — the quirk is not specific to the signed form. Assembly note: `cmp r12, 0FFFFFFFFh` sign-extends to all-ones in 64-bit and never fires; the limit has to come from `mov r11d`) |
| [190](changes/190-wcstoi64/) | `_wcstoi64` (= `wcstoll`) | `ucrtbase.dll!_wcstoi64` | **LANDED** — **1.76x** (up to 2.18x; the overflow limit is SIGN-DEPENDENT — 2^63-1 positive, 2^63 negative — so "-9223372036854775808" is exact (errno 0) while "9223372036854775808" is ERANGE, and the same asymmetry holds in hex. NO 64-BIT DIVISION: the textbook cutoff/cutlim test needs a `div` per call, so `mul` supplies the high half instead and two `jc`s plus one limit compare do the whole guard. Needs one more long-lived value than 188, hence rbp for the accumulator, EIGHT pushes, and 28h rather than 20h of ABI-call padding) |
| [191](changes/191-wcstoui64/) | `_wcstoui64` (= `wcstoull`) | `ucrtbase.dll!_wcstoui64` | **LANDED** — **1.79x** (up to 2.21x; closes the wide parser family — six implementations now cover EIGHT exported names. Change 113's unsigned tail: the limit is 2^64-1 and does NOT move with the sign (the exact opposite of 190), '-' negates modulo 2^64 so "-1" is _UI64_MAX with errno 0 and "-18446744073709551615" is 1, but on OVERFLOW it returns _UI64_MAX regardless of sign rather than a negated magnitude. Because the limit is the full range the post-add limit compare can never fire — kept anyway so 190 and 191 stay one diff apart) |
| [192](changes/192-rtlarebitsclear/) | `RtlAreBitsClear` | `ntdll.dll!RtlAreBitsClear` | **SUPERSEDED by [259](changes/259-rtlarebitsset/)** - as with 030, **the two are the same speed on a shared corpus** (8.0 ns each; live 11.9). Retired for unambiguous provenance, not for speed. Settled by measurement in [audits/superseded-duplicates](audits/superseded-duplicates/), not by comparing headline numbers: both changes and the live export were run over one identical corpus, each side in its OWN process (the two implementations export the same symbol name in two of these groups and cannot be linked together), and all three answer hashes were IDENTICAL. **No defect in the older change** - it is correct, and it is retired only so that one export maps to one implementation |
| [193](changes/193-rtlistextunicode/) | `RtlIsTextUnicode` (encoding heuristic) | `ntdll.dll!RtlIsTextUnicode` | **LANDED** — **5.71x** (up to 8.70x). **The slowest routine found anywhere in this project's headroom survey**: 719 ns for 508 bytes, ~2.9 ns per 16-bit unit. Its cost SATURATES at ~735 ns from 1 KB to 128 KB, because `mov r14d,100h ; cmova` clamps the scan to 256 units — which is precisely why converting it wins at EVERY size at or above 512 bytes, and why that was measured before any work started. **The only change here whose contract could not be derived black-box**: three probe rounds failed to explain ASCII16 (set for 200 units of 'a' but not for 200 of a..z rotating) or STATISTICS (not the zero-byte-parity rule), so the shipped code was read (dumpbin /disasm, RVA 0x000D3A10). Both are TOTAL VARIATION sums of consecutive high/low bytes: ASCII16 iff lo_var<0x7F and hi_var==0, STATISTICS iff 3*hi_var<lo_var — which explains the 64-identical-unit boundary low>3*high exactly, since the only nonzero delta is the first, against 0. Two slots were misread first and the fuzz found both: the post-loop test is on the last HIGH byte (84090 mismatches of 3M, all in NULL_BYTES) and the CR/LF threshold divides by 40 not 10 (magic 0xCCCCCCCD with a TOTAL shift of 37; 8578 mismatches, delta-debugged to 19 x 'a' then one 0x1A). Vectorises because both statistics are |b[j]-b[j-2]| over one byte stream split by parity, so a single load at cursor-2 supplies every predecessor and two masked vpsadbw split and sum them — NO cross-iteration dependency. Bit-exact on the BOOL AND the rewritten *lpi over 21 ask-masks, 1.1M exhaustive short buffers and 400k fuzz. Scope: the DBCS lead-byte pass is out of reach on a DBCS ANSI code page, and the harness CHECKS that bit 0x400 never appears here rather than assuming it |
| [194](changes/194-i64toa-s/) | `_i64toa_s` (bounded 64-bit integer format) | `ucrtbase.dll!_i64toa_s` | **LANDED** — **2.32x** (up to 5.77x; the bounded form of change 057, whose shipped inner loop issues a 64-bit `div` PER DIGIT). The ERANGE path had to be READ, not fitted: no probed rule explained both "1234 size 4 -> 0 '3' '2' '1'" and "-1234 size 2 -> only buf[0] touched". The disassembly (RVA 0x00079D60, worker 0x00076F10) shows why — Buffer[0]=0 is written BEFORE the rest of the validation (so an invalid radix empties the buffer while size 0 leaves it untouched), and `size <= negative+1` fails BEFORE a single digit is emitted; digits go in least-significant-first and are reversed in place at the end, so a buffer that runs out keeps exactly the reversed prefix it held. Ours generates digits into a scratch FIRST so the fit is decided before touching the caller's buffer, then copies forward (success) or BACKWARD (the partial). TWO MEASURED FIXES turned a PARKED first cut into this: every power-of-two radix shifts instead of dividing (radix 2 had been issuing 64 divisions for a 1.00x tie -> 5.77x), and the byte-at-a-time copy that put base 36 at 0.92x became an 8-byte copy that is provably in bounds. Verified on the return, the WHOLE buffer, errno AND the handler count over 16 values x 35 radixes x every size 0..48, plus all 64 powers of two x 35 radixes x every size 1..70 |
| [195](changes/195-ui64toa-s/) | `_ui64toa_s` (bounded unsigned 64-bit format) | `ucrtbase.dll!_ui64toa_s` | **LANDED** — **2.38x** (up to 5.68x; change 194 with `negative` permanently 0, which is not an assumption: the signed entry at RVA 0x00079D60 computes (Radix==10 && Value<0) and calls 0x00079DAC, and the unsigned entry at 0x00079D90 passes a hard zero into the SAME address — the two are four instructions apart in the disassembly. With negative fixed at 0 the early ERANGE test reduces to SizeInChars <= 1) |
| [196](changes/196-i64tow-s/) | `_i64tow_s` (bounded wide 64-bit format) | `ucrtbase.dll!_i64tow_s` | **LANDED** — **2.29x** (up to 5.49x; the wide form. Byte and wide are separate functions at separate addresses, so identical behaviour was MEASURED, not inherited: 200000 random (value,size,radix) triples compared CHARACTER FOR CHARACTER including the untouched cells past the terminator, plus return and errno — 0 differences. SizeInChars counts CHARACTERS; the 2-digit decimal table holds a DWORD per entry so radix 10 still writes two digits per store and divides once per two digits; the copy still moves 8 bytes — four characters — at a time. Within 2 % of the byte form at every class, because the work is dominated by the division chain rather than the stores) |
| [197](changes/197-ui64tow-s/) | `_ui64tow_s` (bounded unsigned wide 64-bit format) | `ucrtbase.dll!_ui64tow_s` | **LANDED** — **2.06x** (up to 3.63x; closes the bounded 64-bit formatter family — 194 byte signed, 195 byte unsigned, 196 wide signed, 197 wide unsigned, all four live-proven together. The narrowest of the four for two visible reasons: `42` base 10 is 1.07x because ucrtbase's own unsigned wide entry is already its fastest small case at 3.61 ns, and 64 bits base 2 is 46.66 ns against 31.34 for the signed wide form — 64 wide digits is 128 bytes of scratch to copy, the one place the wide forms genuinely pay for the extra byte per character) |
| [198](changes/198-itoa-s/) | `_itoa_s` / `_ltoa_s` (bounded 32-bit integer format) | `ucrtbase.dll!_itoa_s` | **LANDED** — **1.73x** (up to 2.82x; the bounded form of change 056 and the 32-bit sibling of 194. ONE implementation, TWO exports: `_ltoa_s` is a separate export the compiler laid out with the branch inverted, and the harness drives BOTH rather than assuming it. The 32-bit width is the whole contract difference — at any radix but 10 the value formats as UNSIGNED 32-bit, so `_itoa_s(-1,b,n,16)` is "ffffffff", eight f's, not 194's sixteen. THREE THINGS WERE MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED: (1) the buffer-fit check is HOISTED via a `maxdig[radix]` table — per-digit bound checks cost ~1.5 cycles each and took base 36 to 0.88x and base 2 from 3.98x to 2.51x; (2) a reciprocal multiply is NOT faster than Zen 4's divider here — the 2^38 magic needing `shrd` measured 0.77x and even the 2^64 magic, whose quotient lands in rdx with no shift at all, only reached 0.95x, so the `div` stayed; (3) the ERANGE path used to generate digits into a scratch and then walk them back out to reproduce ucrtbase's reversed leftover — but the emit loop already runs least-significant-first, which IS that order, so it now writes straight into the caller's buffer and the second pass is gone (0.93x -> 1.07x). ERANGE is a structural tie at best: ~21 ns against ~6 ns for the success classes, because the contract obliges us to CALL ucrtbase's exported `_errno` and `_invalid_parameter_noinfo` where the shipped code reaches the same state internally) |
| [199](changes/199-ultoa-s/) | `_ultoa_s` (bounded unsigned 32-bit format) | `ucrtbase.dll!_ultoa_s` | **LANDED** — **1.77x** (up to 2.84x; change 198 with `negative` permanently 0, kept one diff apart the way 190/191 were. Its base-36 class is what exposed the last real inefficiency in the family: a 32-bit value is at most 7 digits in base 36, so the final copy ALWAYS fell into a counted byte loop, and that loop — not the divides — was the entire deficit against ucrtbase, which reverses in place and never copies. Width-dispatching it (two overlapping 4-byte moves for 4..7 digits, and a branch-free first/last/middle for 1..3) took the class from 10.06 ns to 8.33 ns, 0.93x -> 1.14x) |
| [200](changes/200-itow-s/) | `_itow_s` / `_ltow_s` (bounded wide 32-bit format) | `ucrtbase.dll!_itow_s` | **LANDED** — **1.65x** (up to **3.57x**; the wide form, again ONE implementation for TWO exports with `_ltow_s` patched and driven separately rather than assumed. Base 2 is the widest win in the family: a 32-bit value is 32 digits and every power-of-two radix shifts instead of dividing, against ucrtbase's divide-per-digit. The wide ERANGE class is an honest TIE — repeated runs of the same binary read between 0.94x and 1.05x — and that is recorded in RESULTS.md rather than papered over) |
| [201](changes/201-ultow-s/) | `_ultow_s` (bounded unsigned wide 32-bit format) | `ucrtbase.dll!_ultow_s` | **LANDED** — **1.55x** (up to 2.72x; closes the 32-bit formatter family — FOUR implementations now cover SIX exported names. The tightest of the four: with no sign test and an unsigned magnitude ucrtbase's own two-digit case is already near its floor at 3.62 ns, so the smallest class is a genuine tie rather than a win, and the branch-free 1..3-cell copy is what brought it back over the line) |
| [202](changes/202-convertguidtostringw/) | `ConvertGuidToStringW` (GUID -> `{XXXXXXXX-...}`) | `iphlpapi.dll!ConvertGuidToStringW` | **LANDED** — **37.30x** (up to **110.89x** — the largest win in the project, and the first target from `iphlpapi.dll`. The shipped routine DOES NOT FORMAT THE GUID: it spills the eleven fields as varargs, loads the literal "{%08lX-%04X-%04X-%02X%02X-%02X%02X%02X%02X%02X%02X}" and calls a StringCchPrintfW clone that re-parses that format on EVERY call and dispatches each conversion through a per-character helper — ~305 ns to write 38 characters. The trick is that Data1/2/3 are little-endian integers printed most-significant-nibble first while Data4 prints in memory order, so the sixteen bytes appear in print order 3,2,1,0, 5,4, 7,6, 8..15 — a SINGLE vpshufb — after which the whole conversion is branch-free (nibble split, vpunpck interleave, vpshufb through a hex table, vpmovzxbw to UTF-16) over a pre-stored 39-cell template. AVX2 only. THREE distinct failure lengths had to be reproduced exactly: cch 0 leaves the buffer UNTOUCHED, 1..38 writes a truncated prefix plus a terminator, and cch >= 0x80000000 returns 122 with String[0]=0 rather than 87. THIS IS THE CHANGE THAT FOUND THE ABI BUG — its first cut used xmm6/xmm7 and reported 0.00 ns while being bit-exact, which exposed 16 implementations violating the Win64 register contract; see tools/abi-check) |
| [203](changes/203-convertguidtostringa/) | `ConvertGuidToStringA` (narrow GUID -> `{XXXXXXXX-...}`) | `iphlpapi.dll!ConvertGuidToStringA` | **LANDED** — **35.58x** (up to **97.16x**; the narrow sibling of 202, and the same cause — 263 ns to write 38 characters through a StringCchPrintfA clone that re-parses its format on every call. THE CONTRACT WAS MEASURED, NOT INHERITED: it is a separate export at a different address, and this project has found A/W pairs carrying different conventions before, so probes/cgsa.c drove BOTH over 200k random (GUID, cch) pairs and compared character for character — 0 return-value differences, 0 buffer differences — and settled one ordering detail, that the NULL test precedes the length test so (NULL, NULL, 0) is 87 and not 122. CHEAPER than the wide form: 202 spends a vpmovzxbw pair and two vextracti128 widening 32 ASCII bytes into UTF-16 cells, and here the vpshufb output IS the answer, so the tail is five stores whose last two overlap deliberately on out[29..32] rather than masking a 12-byte run. That also makes the truncating class 14.03x against 202's 10.38x. Both iphlpapi targets are now live-proven in one harness, patched and driven separately) |
| [204](changes/204-rtludiv128/) | `RtlUdiv128` (128/64 unsigned division) | `ntdll.dll!RtlUdiv128` | **LANDED** — **6.66x** (up to **32.79x**; a FLAT ~69 ns per call whatever the operands, because the shipped code at RVA 0x0014A250 is a 64-iteration restoring shift-subtract long division — branch-free inside the loop but always sixty-four passes. x86-64 already HAS this instruction: `div r64` does 128/64 in ~20-40 cycles, and the only reason a software loop exists is that it #DEs when the quotient will not fit in 64 bits — which happens on EXACTLY DividendHigh >= Divisor, so one unsigned compare splits the two regions with zero slack. TWO CLOSED FORMS FOR THE OVERFLOW REGION WERE TRIED AND BOTH ARE WRONG: "saturates to all-ones, remainder lo+d" matches (1,0,1) and every case the first probe picked but fails (7FFFFFFFFFFFFFFF,0,100000000h) where the export gives FFFFFFFF00000000; "the true quotient mod 2^64" explains that one and contradicts the first. Neither holds because the loop's 64-bit remainder register OVERFLOWS — `sar 63` recovers one lost bit for the compare, but bits already shifted off the top of r are gone — so the loop itself is the specification and this implementation reproduces it there rather than inventing a rule. Divisor 0 needs no case of its own: hi >= 0 is always true so it takes the loop and never reaches a div, which is required since the shipped function does not fault on it. The degenerate classes come out 1.13x rather than tied, because rbx/rdi go through push/pop here instead of stores to the caller's shadow space, and they are spilled INSIDE the slow path so the fast path never pays for them. Correctness sweeps the boundary at d-1/d/d+1 — an off-by-one there is a #DE, not a wrong answer — plus an exhaustive block, 12167 edge triples and 1.5M fuzz cases) |
| [205](changes/205-uuidfromstringa/) | `UuidFromStringA` (36-char GUID parse) | `rpcrt4.dll!UuidFromStringA` | **LANDED** — **7.45x** (up to **23.30x**; first target from `rpcrt4.dll`. It measured 82.39 ns against its own WIDE sibling's 23.33 ns for identical work — a 3.5x penalty for the NARROWER input, which is the signature of a wrapper that widens its argument and calls the wide path. THE CONTRACT IS THE OPPOSITE OF ntdll's ON THE ONE DETAIL THAT MATTERS: a braced string is REJECTED here (1705), where change 118's RtlGUIDFromString REQUIRES the braces — getting the pair backwards would be an easy and invisible mistake, since both still return "an error" on what the other accepts. Two more measured surprises: StringUuid == NULL is a SUCCESS returning the nil UUID, not an error and not a fault; and the output GUID must be left UNTOUCHED on failure, which rules out change 118's shape of writing bytes into the caller's GUID as it parses, so this one accumulates into a stack scratch and stores only once the string is good. Validation is BRANCHLESS: a 256-entry table maps hex digits to their value and everything else — NUL and '-' included — to 0FFh, all 32 lookups are OR-ed together, and one `test acc, 0F0h` catches any invalid character because a real nibble only sets bits 0..3. Page-safe: the fast path reads all 37 bytes before knowing the string is that long, so within 37 bytes of a page boundary it walks for the terminator first. Correctness includes an EXHAUSTIVE 36-position x 255-value single-character corruption sweep and a NOACCESS guard with every length 0..38 ending at the boundary) |
| [206](changes/206-stringfromguid2/) | `StringFromGUID2` (GUID -> braced string) | `combase.dll!StringFromGUID2` | **LANDED** — **3.18x** (up to **5.25x**; first target from `combase.dll`, and the most widely used GUID formatter in COM at 11.32 ns a call. Unlike 202 and 203 the shipped code here is NOT a printf engine — 11 ns is a real formatter — which is why the win is 5x and not 100x. Change 202's renderer is reused VERBATIM, and that was verified rather than assumed: "looks like the same format" is not the claim "is the same bytes", so probes/sfg.c rendered 200 000 random GUIDs through both live exports and compared them character by character — 0 differences. TWO CONTRACT TRAPS: (1) there is NO TRUNCATING PATH — cchMax <= 38 returns 0 and writes NOTHING, where 202 writes a truncated prefix for 1..38, so an implementation that borrowed 202's shape wholesale would scribble into a buffer the caller never authorised; (2) cchMax is SIGNED, so the compare must be `jl` not `jb` — an unsigned compare reads -1 as 4294967295 and renders 78 bytes into the caller's buffer, and the test sweeps negatives down to INT_MIN for exactly that reason. The return value is 39, the count INCLUDING the terminator, not 38. Because the length is checked first, a NULL buffer with cchMax 0 returns 0 without faulting — that falls out of the ordering rather than needing its own test) |
| [207](changes/207-iidfromstring/) | `IIDFromString` (braced GUID parse) | `combase.dll!IIDFromString` | **LANDED** — **3.18x** (up to 4.41x; the parse half of the COM GUID path, 32.96 ns for 38 characters. Two reasons, both in the disassembly at RVA 0x000E6C20: the length check is a ONE-CHARACTER-AT-A-TIME strlen that runs before any parsing, and each hex digit costs three range compares. It costs the same whether the string parses or not (32.96 vs 33.14 ns) because that strlen runs either way. THE FAILURE CONTRACT IS THE HARD PART: this function writes into the caller's GUID AS IT PARSES, so a malformed string leaves a PARTIALLY FILLED GUID that must match byte for byte. Rather than decode 200 lines of assembly, probes/wmask.c asked the live function — corrupt exactly one character, report which of the 16 output bytes moved away from a poison fill — and the rule came back exact: every field is stored only once IT AND ITS TRAILING SEPARATOR validate, except Data1 (zeroed then re-stored after EVERY digit, so its partial value is visible: corrupting digit 1 leaves 0, digit 2 leaves 0D, digit 3 leaves 0DE) and Data4[0] (no trailing separator). Data4[7] is stored BEFORE the closing brace is checked, so corrupting '}' leaves all 16 bytes written. TWO ERROR CODES that are not interchangeable: E_INVALIDARG for a structural rejection (NULL output, or length != 38 exactly — nothing written), CO_E_IIDSTRING for a content one. A NULL string is SUCCESS with the nil GUID. Ours replaces the strlen with one AVX2 pass and each digit with a table lookup whose validity accumulator puts the high byte in bits 8+ and the table's 0FFh in bits 4..7, so one test covers both. Correctness is a 38-position x 255-value corruption sweep plus 38 x 12 wide characters above 0xFF — that sweep is what pins the partial-write table, since each position stops the parser at a different field boundary) |
| [208](changes/208-uuidfromstringw/) | `UuidFromStringW` (wide 36-char GUID parse) | `rpcrt4.dll!UuidFromStringW` | **LANDED** — **2.41x** (up to **9.87x**; the wide sibling of 205, and the function that was doing the real work all along — 23.33 ns, a third of what the narrow form cost before 205, because the narrow one was widening its input and calling this. The contract is 205's character for character, and that was VERIFIED: probes/ufs.c drove both live exports over 200 000 generated strings and found 0 return-value and 0 output differences. THE WIDTH IS PAID FOR BY SATURATION, not by reading 32 words: the 36 cells are narrowed to bytes with three vpackuswb and then it is 205's byte parser verbatim. That is sound precisely BECAUSE vpackuswb saturates — 0100h-7FFFh clamp to 0FFh and 8000h-FFFFh are NEGATIVE so they clamp to 00h, both invalid in the hex table, and nothing but 002Dh can become '-'. So U+0130 does not become '0' and U+802D does not become '-'. The one thing saturation WOULD break is the terminator test, since 8000h collapses to 00h and would look like a NUL — so the length is checked on the ORIGINAL wide data with vpcmpeqw before any narrowing. Correctness includes a 36 x 17 sweep over characters above 0xFF containing exactly those traps, and the page-guard fallback narrows scalar-wise because the SIMD narrow reads 80 bytes where the bounded walk only proved 74 readable) |
| [209](changes/209-lstrcpynw/) | `lstrcpynW` (bounded wide copy) | `kernelbase.dll!lstrcpynW` | **LANDED** — **3.94x** (up to **12.58x**, 42.65 GB/s against 3.37; MAX_PATH-shaped copies, which is what almost every real caller does, run 10.20x. Unlike the two shlwapi routines the same survey RULED OUT there is no semantic excuse here — no folding, no collation, just a bounded copy that stops at a NUL. THE FAULT CASE SHAPES EVERYTHING: this function SWALLOWS A FAULTING SOURCE, returning NULL with the readable prefix already copied, and that forces two things a naive vectorised copy gets wrong. (1) The copy must be PAGE-SAFE, not merely wide: a 32-byte load straddling a page end faults BEFORE storing, so a chunked copy would leave FEWER characters behind than the shipped byte-at-a-time loop does. (2) THE SOURCE IS READ BEFORE THE BOUND IS TESTED — measured, not guessed: with n-1 exactly equal to the source length the shipped loop reads src[n-1], ONE PAST the last character it copies, and faults there. probes/pg.c caught it as eleven failures at precisely that relationship, where a bound-first loop terminated cleanly and returned the destination instead of NULL. The exception is caught in seh.c, a __try/__except that on x64 is table-driven and costs the fast path nothing. Also measured: the destination is TERMINATED NOT PADDED ("ab" into n=10 leaves cells 2..9 untouched, ruling out a strncpy shape), n==0 writes nothing at all, and n is used UNSIGNED so -1 copies everything. Live-proven with 24 000 of 120 000 cases run against a NOACCESS guard page) |
| [210](changes/210-comparestringordinal/) | `CompareStringOrdinal` (ordinal wide compare, both modes) | `kernelbase.dll!CompareStringOrdinal` | **LANDED** — **3.19x** over ten rows, worst row **1.24x** (up to **5.08x**, 68 GB/s; the API Microsoft recommends for non-linguistic comparison, so it is on a great many hot paths). THE CONTRACT BEING REPRODUCIBLE WAS NOT A GIVEN: the same survey abandoned StrCmpNW (orders linguistically) and StrChrIW (3236-member equivalence classes from ignorable code points), so probes/cso.c measured this one — case-sensitive is a pure code-unit compare (0 differences over 300k pairs), the ignore-case classes are 1-2 members, the fold is EXACTLY RtlUpcaseUnicodeChar (65534 code units, 0 mismatches), it ORDERS BY THE UPCASED VALUES ("a" vs "B" is LESS where raw code units say GREATER, 0 differences over 400k), and it is locale-independent including tr-TR and lt-LT. THE IGNORE-CASE PATH RESTS ON ONE OBSERVATION: upcase is a FUNCTION, so a == b implies upcase(a) == upcase(b) — a chunk equal RAW needs no folding at all. The first cut folded unconditionally and fell to a per-character table lookup above 0x7F, measuring 1303 ns against the shipped 596 on 4000 Cyrillic characters (0.46x); three tiers took that to 122 ns (4.86x) and ASCII ignore-case from 1.63x to 4.90x. Short strings needed their own vector path too — 13 characters fell into a scalar tail at 0.85x, and two OVERLAPPING 8-character compares (both windows inside min(c1,c2), and the trailing tzcnt is still the FIRST difference because the leading window proved [0,8) equal) took it to 2.15x. Nothing is pushed: c1, c2 and the scalar stop index live in the caller's shadow space. Also measured: a count is EXACT so embedded NULs compare as ordinary characters, only -1 means NUL-terminated |
| [211](changes/211-lstrcpyna/) | `lstrcpynA` (bounded narrow copy) | `kernelbase.dll!lstrcpynA` | **LANDED** — **7.31x** (up to **67.88x**, 115.65 GB/s). THE SURVEY GAVE THIS ONE AWAY: 4000 NARROW characters cost ~2372 ns and 4000 WIDE ones ~2375 — one per-character loop whose cost does not depend on the width, so the narrow form moves half the bytes for the same money.
